## Languages
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Polynesia&action=edit&section=28 "Edit section: Languages")]
Main article: [Polynesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_languages "Polynesian languages")
Polynesian languages are all members of the family of [Oceanic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_languages "Oceanic languages"), a sub-branch of the [Austronesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages "Austronesian languages") language family. Polynesian languages show a considerable degree of similarity. The [vowels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vowels "Vowels") are generally the same—/a/, /e/, /i/, /o/, and /u/, pronounced as in [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_language "Italian language"), [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language "Spanish language"), and [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language "German language")—and the consonants are always followed by a vowel. The languages of various island groups show changes in [consonants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consonant "Consonant"). The [glottal stop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glottal_stop "Glottal stop") /ʔ/ is increasingly represented by an inverted comma or [ʻokina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BBokina "ʻokina"). In the [Society Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_Islands "Society Islands"), the original [Proto-Polynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Polynesian_language "Proto-Polynesian language") _*k_ and _*[ŋ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voiced_velar_nasal "Voiced velar nasal")_ (or the ng sound) have merged as /ʔ/, _*s_ changed to /h/, and _*w_ changed to /v/; so the name for the ancestral homeland, deriving from Proto-Nuclear Polynesian _*sawaiki_ ,[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-pollexSawaiki-91) becomes Havaiʻi. In New Zealand, where _*s_ changed to /h/, the ancient home is [Hawaiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiki "Hawaiki"). In the Cook Islands, where /ʔ/ replaces _*s_ (with a likely intermediate stage of _*h_) and /v/ replaces _*w_ , it is ʻAvaiki. In the Hawaiian islands, where /ʔ/ and /h/ replace _*k_ and _*s_ , respectively, the largest island of the group is named [Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawai%CA%BBi_\(island\) "Hawaiʻi \(island\)"). In Samoa, where /v/ and /ʔ/ replace _*w_ and _*k_ , respectively, the largest island is called [Savaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savai%CA%BBi "Savaiʻi").[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-trh-2)
[Maritime Polynesian Pidgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_Polynesian_Pidgin "Maritime Polynesian Pidgin") based on Tahitian, Māori and Hawaiian was a common regional [trade language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_language "Trade language") used between European voyagers and local islanders between 18th to 19th centuries before supplanted by English.[[89]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-92)
## Economy
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Polynesia&action=edit&section=29 "Edit section: Economy")]
With the exception of New Zealand, the majority of independent Polynesian islands derive much of their income from foreign aid and remittances from those who live in other countries. Some encourage their young people to go where they can earn good money to remit to their stay-at-home relatives. Many Polynesian locations, such as [Easter Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Island "Easter Island"), supplement this with tourism income. Some have more unusual sources of income, such as [Tuvalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuvalu "Tuvalu") which marketed its '[.tv](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/.tv ".tv")' internet top-level domain name or the Cooks that relied on [postage stamp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postage_stamp "Postage stamp") sales. 
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b6/Tuilaepa_Sailele_Malielegaoi.jpg/250px-Tuilaepa_Sailele_Malielegaoi.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tuilaepa_Sailele_Malielegaoi.jpg)[Tuilaepa Sailele Malielegaoi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuilaepa_Sailele_Malielegaoi "Tuilaepa Sailele Malielegaoi"), [Prime Minister of Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Samoa "Prime Minister of Samoa") from 1998 to 2021, who initiated the [Polynesian Leaders Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_Leaders_Group "Polynesian Leaders Group") in late 2011.
Aside from New Zealand, another focus area of economic dependence regarding tourism is Hawaii. Hawaii is one of the most visited areas within the Polynesian Triangle, entertaining more than ten million visitors annually, excluding 2020. The economy of Hawaii, like that of New Zealand, is steadily dependent on annual tourists and financial counseling or aid from other countries or states. "The rate of tourist growth has made the economy overly dependent on this one sector, leaving Hawaii extremely vulnerable to external economic forces."[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-93) By keeping this in mind, island states and nations similar to Hawaii are paying closer attention to other avenues that can positively affect their economy by practicing more independence and less emphasis on tourist entertainment. 
## Inter-Polynesian cooperation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Polynesia&action=edit&section=30 "Edit section: Inter-Polynesian cooperation")]
The first major attempt at uniting the Polynesian islands was by [Imperial Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan") in the 1930s, when various theorists (chiefly [Hachirō Arita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hachir%C5%8D_Arita "Hachirō Arita")) began promulgating the idea of what would soon become known as the [Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_East_Asia_Co-Prosperity_Sphere "Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere"). Under the Greater East Asia Co-Prosperity Sphere, all nations stretching from [Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia "Southeast Asia") and [Northeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Asia "Northeast Asia") to [Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceania "Oceania") would be united under one, large, cultural and economic bloc which would be free from Western [imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism "Imperialism"). The policy theorists who conceived it, along with the Japanese public, largely saw it as a pan-Asian movement driven by ideals of freedom and independence from Western colonial oppression. In practice, however, it was frequently corrupted by militarists who saw it as an effective policy vehicle through which to strengthen Japan's position and advance its dominance within Asia. At its greatest extent, it stretched from [Japanese occupied Indochina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_coup_d%27%C3%A9tat_in_French_Indochina "Japanese coup d'état in French Indochina") in the west to the [Gilbert Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilbert_Islands "Gilbert Islands") in the east, although it was originally planned to stretch as far east as [Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii "Hawaii") and [Easter Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Island "Easter Island") and as far west as [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India "India"). This never came to fruition, however, as Japan was defeated during [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II") and subsequently lost all power and influence it had.[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-94)[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-Weinberg-95)
After several years of discussing a potential regional grouping, three sovereign states (Samoa, Tonga, and Tuvalu) and five self-governing but non-sovereign territories formally launched, in November 2011, the [Polynesian Leaders Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_Leaders_Group "Polynesian Leaders Group"), intended to cooperate on a variety of issues including culture and language, education, responses to climate change, and trade and investment. It does not, however, constitute a political or monetary union.[[93]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-96)[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-97)[[95]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-Savali-98)
## Navigation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Polynesia&action=edit&section=31 "Edit section: Navigation")]
Main article: [Polynesian navigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_navigation "Polynesian navigation")
Polynesia comprised islands diffused throughout a triangular area with sides of four thousand miles. The area from the Hawaiian Islands in the north, to Easter Island in the east and to New Zealand in the south were all settled by Polynesians. 
[Navigators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navigator "Navigator") traveled to small inhabited islands using only their own senses and knowledge passed by [oral tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_tradition "Oral tradition") from navigator to apprentice. In order to locate directions at various times of day and year, navigators in Eastern Polynesia memorized important facts: the motion of specific [stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star "Star"), and where they would rise on the [horizon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horizon "Horizon") of the ocean; [weather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weather "Weather"); times of travel; wildlife species (which congregate at particular positions); directions of swells on the ocean, and how the crew would feel their motion; colors of the sea and sky, especially how clouds would cluster at the locations of some islands; and angles for approaching harbors. 
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3e/Priests_traveling_across_kealakekua_bay_for_first_contact_rituals.jpg/250px-Priests_traveling_across_kealakekua_bay_for_first_contact_rituals.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Priests_traveling_across_kealakekua_bay_for_first_contact_rituals.jpg)Polynesian (Hawaiian) navigators sailing multi-hulled [canoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canoe "Canoe"), c. 1781 [![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6d/Va%27a_canoe%2C_Matavai_village%2C_Savaii%2C_Samoa_MS.JPG/250px-Va%27a_canoe%2C_Matavai_village%2C_Savaii%2C_Samoa_MS.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Va%27a_canoe,_Matavai_village,_Savaii,_Samoa_MS.JPG)A common fishing canoe _va'a_ with outrigger in [Savaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savai%CA%BBi "Savaiʻi") island, [Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoa "Samoa"), 2009
These [wayfinding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayfinding "Wayfinding") techniques, along with [outrigger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outrigger "Outrigger") [canoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canoe "Canoe") construction methods, were kept as [guild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guild "Guild") secrets. Generally, each island maintained a guild of navigators who had very high status; in times of famine or difficulty these navigators could trade for aid or evacuate people to neighboring islands. On his first voyage of Pacific exploration Cook had the services of a Polynesian navigator, [Tupaia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tupaia_\(navigator\) "Tupaia \(navigator\)"), who drew a hand-drawn chart of the islands within 3,200 km (2,000 mi) radius (to the north and west) of his home island of [Ra'iatea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ra%27iatea "Ra'iatea"). Tupaia had knowledge of 130 islands and named 74 on his chart.[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-Druett2-99) Tupaia had navigated from Ra'iatea in short voyages to 13 islands. He had not visited western Polynesia, as since his grandfather's time the extent of voyaging by Raiateans has diminished to the islands of eastern Polynesia. His grandfather and father had passed to Tupaia the knowledge as to the location of the major islands of western Polynesia and the navigation information necessary to voyage to [Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoa "Samoa"), [Tonga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonga "Tonga") and Melanesian island of [Fiji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiji "Fiji").[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-Druett1-100) As the Admiralty orders directed Cook to search for the ["Great Southern Continent"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terra_Australis "Terra Australis"), Cook ignored Tupaia's chart and his skills as a navigator. To this day, original traditional methods of Polynesian Navigation are still taught in the [Polynesian outlier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_outlier "Polynesian outlier") of [Taumako Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duff_Islands "Duff Islands") in the [Solomon Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islands_of_the_Solomon_Islands "Islands of the Solomon Islands"). 
From a single chicken bone recovered from the archaeological site of El Arenal-1, on the [Arauco Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arauco_Peninsula "Arauco Peninsula"), Chile, a 2007 research report looking at radiocarbon dating and an ancient DNA sequence indicate that Polynesian navigators may have reached the Americas at least 100 years before Columbus (who arrived 1492 AD), introducing chickens to South America.[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-101)[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-102) A later report looking at the same specimens concluded: 
> A published, apparently pre-Columbian, Chilean specimen and six pre-European Polynesian specimens also cluster with the same European/Indian subcontinental/Southeast Asian sequences, providing no support for a Polynesian introduction of chickens to South America. In contrast, sequences from two archaeological sites on Easter Island group with an uncommon haplogroup from Indonesia, Japan, and China and may represent a genetic signature of an early Polynesian dispersal. Modeling of the potential marine carbon contribution to the Chilean archaeological specimen casts further doubt on claims for pre-Columbian chickens, and definitive proof will require further analyses of ancient DNA sequences and radiocarbon and stable isotope data from archaeological excavations within both Chile and Polynesia.[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-103)
Knowledge of the traditional Polynesian methods of navigation was largely lost after contact with and colonization by Europeans. This left the problem of accounting for the presence of the Polynesians in such isolated and scattered parts of the Pacific. By the late 19th century to the early 20th century, a more generous view of Polynesian navigation had come into favor, perhaps creating a romantic picture of their canoes, seamanship and navigational expertise. 
In the mid to late 1960s, scholars began testing sailing and paddling experiments related to Polynesian navigation: [David Lewis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Henry_Lewis "David Henry Lewis") sailed his catamaran from Tahiti to New Zealand using [stellar navigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celestial_navigation "Celestial navigation") without instruments and [Ben Finney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Finney "Ben Finney") built a 12-meter (40-foot) replica of a Hawaiian double canoe "Nalehia" and tested it in Hawaii.[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-104) Meanwhile, Micronesian ethnographic research in the Caroline Islands revealed that traditional stellar navigational methods were still in everyday use. Recent re-creations of Polynesian voyaging, as done by the Honolulu-based [Polynesian Voyaging Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_Voyaging_Society "Polynesian Voyaging Society"), have used methods based largely on Micronesian methods and the teachings of Micronesian navigator [Mau Piailug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mau_Piailug "Mau Piailug"). 
It is probable that the Polynesian navigators employed a whole range of techniques including use of the stars, the movement of ocean currents and wave patterns, the air and sea interference patterns caused by islands and [atolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atoll "Atoll"), the flight of birds, the winds and the weather. Scientists think that long-distance Polynesian voyaging followed the seasonal paths of [birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird_migration "Bird migration"). There are some references in their oral traditions to the flight of birds and some say that there were range marks onshore pointing to distant islands in line with these [flyways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flyway "Flyway"). One theory is that they would have taken a [frigatebird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frigatebird "Frigatebird") with them. These birds refuse to land on the water as their feathers will become waterlogged making it impossible to fly. When the voyagers thought they were close to land they may have released the bird, which would either fly towards land or else return to the canoe. It is likely that the Polynesians also used wave and swell formations to navigate. It is thought that the Polynesian navigators may have measured the time it took to sail between islands in "canoe-days" or a similar type of expression.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_]
Another navigational technique may have involved following sea turtle migrations. While other navigational techniques may have been sufficient to reach known islands, some research finds only sea turtles could have helped Polynesian navigators reach new islands. Sea turtle migrations are feasible for canoes to follow, at shallow depths, slower speeds, and in large groups. This could explain how Polynesians were able to find and settle the majority of Pacific Islands.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-105)
Also, people of the Marshall Islands used special devices called [stick charts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Islands_stick_chart "Marshall Islands stick chart"), showing the places and directions of swells and wave-breaks, with tiny seashells affixed to them to mark the positions of islands along the way. Materials for these maps were readily available on beaches, and their making was simple; however, their effective use needed years and years of study.[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia#cite_note-106)
